...that which concerns the manner of volatilizing <term>Alkalis</term> <note>Alkali: A substance that neutralizes acids. In alchemy, these were often fixed salts derived from plants or minerals believed to hold medicinal power.</note>, according to the Precepts of <term>Van Helmont</term> <note>Jan Baptist van Helmont (1580–1644) was a pioneering Flemish chemist and physician who bridge the gap between alchemy and modern chemistry. He is famous for coining the word "gas."</note>: And through these two, one will have the Revelation of all the Mysteries of this famous Author. The first unveiling the <term>Arcana</term> <note>Arcanum (plural Arcana): A secret or mysterious remedy, often considered a hidden "soul" or virtue of a substance.</note>, and the last the substitutes or approximations. But if the difficulties of the one demand excellent Artists <note>In this context, an "Artist" is a skilled practitioner of the alchemical art.</note>; the ease of the other supposes only mediocre ones. And both, consequently, will be able to satisfy everyone.

> *Learn of a certain solvent, which is homogeneous and immutable, dissolving its objects into a liquid primary matter: and you will have obtained the innermost essences of things, and be able to inspect their gifts.* <note>original Latin: "Discite dissolvens aliquod, quod sit homogeneum, immutabile, dissolvens sua objecta in materiam primam liquidam: & nactus eris intimas rerum essentias, harumque dotes posse inspicere."</note>
>
> *But if you do not attain that Arcanum of fire, learn at least to make the <term>salt of Tartar</term> <note>Salt of Tartar: Potassium carbonate, typically prepared by heating the sediment (tartar) found in wine barrels. Making it "volatile" was a major goal in iatrochemistry (chemically-based medicine).</note> volatile, so that by its means you may perfect your dissolutions. Which, although it carries its dissolved substances in <term>equal parts</term> <note>original: "anatice," a medical term meaning "in equal portions."</note> as homogeneous substances when digested within us: it has nevertheless borrowed some of their powers, which it carries inward as the conquerors of most diseases.* <note>original Latin: "Quod si autem ad istud ignis Arcanum non pertingatis, discite saltem, salem Tartari reddere volatilem, ut hujus medio vestras dissolutiones perficiatis. Qui etsi sua soluta, anatice homogenea deferat, digestus in nobis: illorum tamen aliquot vires mutuatus est, quas intro defert, plurimorum morborum domitrices."</note>

# PREFACE

<image-desc>A large ornamental drop cap 'L' enclosed in a square frame with a lattice and floral background pattern.</image-desc>

L<insert>ES</insert> Prefaces that are written on the occasion of the Works of others require three principal things from those who produce them. They require a favorable discourse on the excellence and utility of these Works; they require that nothing be forgotten regarding the merit of their Author; and that one gives an account to the Reader of the motives for publishing them.

He who brought to light the posthumous <note>Posthumous: A work published after the death of the author.</note> Treatise on the **Alkaest** <note>Alkaest: In alchemy, this was the "universal solvent," a substance capable of dissolving any solid matter into its basic liquid form without altering its essential properties.</note> liquor composed by **George Starkey** <note>George Starkey (1628–1665): An influential American-born alchemist and physician who practiced in London and is widely believed to be the man behind the famous alchemical pseudonym Eirenaeus Philalethes.</note>, of which the Translation is given in this Col- <note>original: "Re-". This is a catchword or truncated word, likely referring to a *Recueil* (Collection) or *Registre* (Register).</note>

# P R E F A C E

one can attribute them to him with justice.

If the capacity of a Master is not always convincing proof of that of the Disciple, it is at least a moral reason for some degree of excellence in those whom he has taught. For it is certain that our minds always retain some trace of the impression they have received from our Preceptors <note>Tutors or teachers</note>, however weak a genius we may possess. But if we can say this regarding the speculative Studies of the Schools <note>Refers to Scholasticism, the traditional medieval method of learning based on logic and philosophy</note>, which are founded almost entirely upon opinions that we are permitted to take up or leave as we wish, what more advantageous consequence can we draw regarding <term>Chemical Philosophy</term> <note>The early modern term for chemistry and alchemy, viewing it as a holistic study of nature's secrets</note>? Since this practical Science is founded only upon experiments and upon facts, in which one is instructed by the eyes and by the ears, and since the principles one forms from them

<column-break/>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface, contrasting dogmatic authority with the clarity of empirical or "sensible" philosophy.</meta>

->P R E F A C E<-

remain constant and unshakable. The former are imperious, demanding that they be believed on their word alone and that we receive as certain that which they cannot even make us understand; the latter, on the contrary, receives our consent without demanding it, insofar as it proposes nothing to us that is not evident and palpable. Its dogmas are so many axioms <note>Self-evident truths that require no further proof.</note> which need no proof other than themselves. Thus, we cannot contradict them without feeling, at the same time, the remorse of our own conscience.

This is why an excellent Master, provided he finds even a little natural talent in the subject he instructs, always forms—with a kind of necessity—an excellent Disciple in this <term>sensible Philosophy</term> <note>A philosophy based on sensory experience and observable evidence rather than abstract speculation.</note>. So it is that if the Master happens to be skillful, and the Disciple has the right disposition, one might, it seems to me—

<summary>The author argues that empirical philosophy is superior to dogmatism because it relies on self-evident truths and sensory evidence rather than blind faith. He suggests that a skilled teacher can easily produce an excellent student when working with these clear principles.</summary>

<keywords>sensible philosophy, pedagogy, axioms, evidence, conscience, master and disciple, empiricism</keywords>

seems to conclude quite rightly from this, that the latter is skillful, since he was instructed by the former. I could confirm what I say by countless examples that the Arts could furnish me, where one has almost always seen the great masters produce excellent Disciples: but what use would this proof be in a matter as clear as that?

George Starkey having therefore had the famous Philalethes <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes, a legendary and pseudonymous alchemist whose identity was a major mystery of the 17th century.</note> for a Master—that is to say, that illustrious unknown whose writings give so much light and so much clarity to hermetic Philosophy <term>Hermetic Philosophy: the study of alchemy and occult sciences attributed to Hermes Trismegistus</term>, that the brilliance of it dazzles the minds of the Readers, even to the point of making them fail to recognize the Truth. They are unable to understand how she <note>Truth is personified here as a woman.</note> could have allowed a man to expose her entirely naked in public, in the very place where she has always been the most hidden. That is to say, this great Artist, whose learned writings are the delights of the Disciples of Nature <note>A term used for alchemists who sought to understand and replicate the natural processes of creation and transformation.</note>:

That Starkey was therefore the <insert>^</insert> Disciple of such a master; but that he was also an Adept Philosopher <note>An "Adept" refers to a chemist or alchemist who has successfully discovered the "Philosopher's Stone" or the secret of transmutation.</note>, in addition to what I have just reported which proves the first quality, I believe that his own testimony to prove both, far from being suspect, will alone be sufficient to persuade the judicious and reasonable Reader.

<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding the identity and authorship of the works attributed to Eirenaeus Philalethes, specifically addressing the confusion between different individuals using the same pseudonym.</meta>

-># P R E F A C E<-

In truth, upon a simple reading of this Preface, you would say that it speaks of two Philalethes <note>The name "Philalethes" is Greek for "Lover of Truth," a common pseudonym in 17th-century alchemical literature.</note>, that is to say, of two unknown Persons who took this same name; one of whom was a friend of Starkey <note>George Starkey (1628–1665), an American colonial alchemist and physician who acted as the agent for the mysterious Philalethes.</note> and the Author of the two Poems; and the other who was known only to that Friend, and who was the one we ordinarily understand to be the true Philalethes: that is, the Author of the Book entitled *The Open Entrance* <note>original: "Introitus apertus," referring to the famous alchemical treatise *Introitus apertus ad occlusum regis palatium* (An Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King).</note>. What further encourages this doubt is that the account one reads there of the adventures of the Author of the Poems is somewhat different from the one found in the *Open Entrance*. But on the other hand, if one considers the style and the reasoning of these Poems, one will judge by the conformity they have with those of the other Works attributed to the true Philalethes, that they are truly by him: and that the different accounts found in the

<summary>This page examines the historical debate over whether the alchemical works of "Philalethes" were written by one man or two distinct individuals, comparing the biographical details in his poems to his famous treatise.</summary>
<keywords>Eirenaeus Philalethes, George Starkey, Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King, alchemical authorship, pseudonyms, 17th-century alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface's investigation into the true identity of the alchemist known as Eirenaeus Philalethes, specifically examining the relationship between the author and George Starkey.</meta>

# PREFACE

<column-break/>

Poems, and these multiplied "Philalethes" <note>The name Philalethes means "Lover of Truth" in Greek; it was a common pseudonym for alchemists seeking to remain anonymous.</note> are nothing but feints coordinated between Philalethes and Starkey for fear of being discovered. Starkey, whether by design or without thinking, gives us the proof of this feint himself. For in the Preface to the first of these Poems, he makes his Friend the Author of the two Poems, and even says that he had composed them at his request. And when, in the same place, he provides the list of the works of the true Philalethes, he ranks alongside the *Open Entrance* <note>original: "Introitus apertus," referring to the famous alchemical treatise "Introitus apertus ad occlusum regis palatium" (An Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King).</note> another book which he titles the *Kabbalah of the Wise* <note>original: "Cabala Sapientium."</note>. Now, the Author of the two Poems, in the first book of the second part, acknowledges this Treatise of the *Kabbalah of the Wise* as his own work. From which it follows, according to Starkey himself, that the Author of the *Kabbalah of the Wise*—being the Author of the *Open Entrance* and of the Poems—was the true Philalethes, and the one whom he calls his Friend.

->A ij<-

<summary>This page argues that the various pseudonyms used by the alchemist Philalethes were a coordinated deception involving George Starkey, using internal bibliographic evidence to link their works.</summary>
<keywords>Eirenaeus Philalethes, George Starkey, Open Entrance, Kabbalah of the Wise, alchemical pseudonyms, authorship disputes</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding the true identity of the author Eirenaeus Philalethes and his relationship with George Starkey, following the previous arguments about the authorship of certain alchemical poems.</meta>

# P R E F A C E

Another irrefutable proof is that the Author of the *Commentaries on Ripley* <note>Sir George Ripley (c. 1415–1490) was a famous English alchemist; these commentaries are a foundational text in the "Philalethes" corpus.</note> is unquestionably the true Philalethes. Now, in the Preface to these *Commentaries*, he acknowledges the two Poems as his own Works. Starkey could not have been ignorant of this, since he admits that he had read these *Commentaries*. Thus, the two different persons mentioned by Starkey in his Preface are a fiction, both being one and the same: his Friend, the famous Philalethes.

The other difficulty may be more easily clarified. Starkey, in truth, is not clearly named in the two Prefaces of the Poems. But besides the fact that his style makes him only too recognizable, he signed them both with Latin Anagrams which contain his name. The first ends with this one: *Egregius Christo* <note>Latin: "Excellent in Christ."</note>; which, by the simple transposition of the

Among the proofs that I could bring forward, I will produce only the following, which will, I believe, render the matter incontestable.

Van Helmont, in the 7th chapter of *Lithiasis* <note>A treatise on the "stone" or calculus in the human body</note>, paragraph 22, describes the calcination <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures to achieve decomposition or change in texture</note> of the *Ludus* <note>A specific calcareous stone used in Paracelsian medicine</note> of Paracelsus in these terms: *Let the Ludus be ground into a powder, etc. But let there be added to it the Circulated Salt, of which Paracelsus speaks in his book on Renovation and Restoration, etc.* <note>original: "Teratur Ludus in pulverem &c. Sed addatur illi Sal circulatum , de quo Paracelsus libro de Renovatione & Restauratione. &c."</note> Now, if one considers what Paracelsus says of the Circulated Salt <term>Sal Circulatum: a high-level alchemical solvent or "circulated" salt intended to dissolve stones in the body</term> in the book where Van Helmont refers the reader for instruction, one will recognize that if this latter author had read the 10th book of the *Archidoxes* <note>One of Paracelsus's major works on the "Archidoxes" or "Primary Teachings" of medicine and alchemy</note>, he would have fallen into a considerable lapse of memory by sending the reader to a place where he could learn nothing but the mere name of the Circulated Salt. This is especially true since he could have fully instructed the reader on the manner of preparing it by referring him instead to the third chapter of the 10th Book of the *Archidoxes*.

years of retirement spent in the serious application of experiments on natural things, having recognized how little fruit the Galenic method <note>The traditional medical system based on the four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) as taught by the Greek physician Galen.</note> had produced up until his time, he became disgusted with it. He believed that it was necessary to take a path entirely different from that of other physicians if one wished to find anything useful for the healing of the diseases of the human body. The example of Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a pioneer of the "medical revolution" who emphasized chemistry and observation over ancient texts.</note>—who had distinguished himself from the common crowd and had attracted the great reputation he acquired only by this means—opened the way for him. The writings of this extraordinary man, whose obscurity and novelty gave others cause for mockery, gave him instead cause for admiration and veneration; they soon finished facilitating for him all the openings that this undertaking required.

He then imagined that this confused mixture flowed further down; the veins of the **Mesentery** <note>The fold of membrane that attaches the intestines to the abdominal wall and holds them in place.</note> having attracted or sucked up the most liquid part through the almost infinite number of their small mouths; the thickest remainder stays separated in the **intestines** as if in a filter, to continue its path there until the seat <note>The rectum or anus.</note>. These ideas must necessarily be the cause of our health or of our diseases. For if it happens that they are regular and that they contain only the proper traces of the laudable functions of our body's organs, nothing can follow but perfect health, or the integrity of life; just as, on the contrary, if these ideas are extravagant and opposed to the goal of the laudable functions of our organs, the result will be the alteration of the integrity of life, or Diseases.

Since only those who have taken part in this <term>convention<note>The author views language as a "social contract" or informal agreement between people to use specific sounds for specific meanings.</note></term> can use these words to represent a specific idea, it is necessary for those who wish to know the meaning of the words or sounds that certain men use among themselves to communicate their thoughts, to learn from these same men the idea they have attached to the words they use. This is why, if I wish to instruct myself in the language of a Nation, an Art, or a Science, I must above all else inquire of those who compose that Nation, or profess that Art or Science, regarding the idea they have linked to the words or sounds they use to make themselves understood.

Common Mercury <note>original: "Le Mercure commun" — liquid quicksilver as found in nature, as opposed to "philosophical mercury."</note> in its entirety, its foreign parts, nor its essential parts, cannot therefore be the matter of the Alkaest <note>The "Alkaest" was a hypothetical universal solvent sought by alchemists, capable of reducing any body to its primary matter.</note> according to Van-Helmont: if it is true that Mercury enters into the Alkaest, it must enter there with other materials, which is still impossible according to him. For whatever change or alteration appears in Mercury in these types of mixtures, it is not a real change, as it would need to be to become Alkaest; but it is an apparent and fantastic change.

Therefore, the vital spirit is saline and thus balsamic, and a guardian against corruption. <note>original: "Est ergo spiritus vitalis salinus ideoque balsamicus, & custos à corruptione."</note> (The Spirit of Life.) However, the spirit of human urine is neither acid nor alkaline.* <note>original: "Spiritus autem lotii humani nec est acidus nec alcalizatus." This reflects the author's attempt to categorize ammonia outside the standard acid-base binary of the time.</note>

Whether by chance or by design, the name "Urine" was derived *from burning* <note>original Latin: "ab urendo"</note>; no other name could have suited it better. ->of the Secrets of Philosophy.<-

When JESUS CHRIST said that one must not give holy things to dogs, nor scatter pearls before swine <note>Matthew 7:6</note>, he did not mean by that the secrets of Physics <term>Physics: in this context, the study of natural laws and alchemy</term> or of Medicine, but rather fraternal correction. It is useless to offer such correction to the furious and the brutal, who, far from being capable of receiving it to their benefit, would take the opportunity to destroy those who gave it to them. *Do not give what is holy to dogs, nor cast your pearls before swine, lest they trample them under their feet, and turn and tear you in pieces.* <note>original Latin: "Nolite dare sanctum canibus, neque mittatis margaritas vestras ante porcos. Could one say that a man who had oil for the maintenance of a lamp, and who would not wish to supply it, was less guilty of its extinction than he who would extinguish it by blowing upon it? No, without doubt. I say the same: that those who possess <term>secrets of Remedies</term> <note>In this period, "secrets" referred to proprietary medical formulas or specific chemical preparations not yet common knowledge.</note> which can cure or relieve the sick, and let those patients die without relieving them, are no less guilty than he who would have killed them. # PREFACE.

->to prevent birth is a form of destruction.<- <note>original: "dium est prohibere nasci." This is a fragment of a Latin quote, likely from Tertullian: "impedimentum genesios est prohibere nasci," meaning "to prevent birth is to anticipate murder."</note> It seems to me that one could say with much more reason, that it is a form of homicide to take life from those who already possess it, by withholding—through a refusal to share—the very things that could preserve and continue that life.

Not only <term>Christian charity</term> <note>The theological virtue of selfless love and care for one's neighbor</note>, but pure <term>humanity</term> <note>The secular or natural moral obligation toward fellow human beings</note>, obliges us to relieve those who are afflicted by illness or infirmity. We are all weak and destitute; we all have need of one another. Thus, if we wish to be helped in our own times of necessity, we must not refuse the same grace to those who have need of us.

But it will happen, perhaps, that reasons of self-interest will outweigh those of charity, and it will be said that the building of a <term>fortune</term> <note>In this context, the accumulation of personal wealth or professional status</note> is not contrary to this virtue. # SEVERAL PASSAGES taken from the English Works of Philalethes, where he speaks of the Alkaest, and translated into English. <note>original: "mis en François" - the manuscript collector translated these from English to French; they are here returned to English.</note>

->I,<-

*Extract from the eleventh Chapter of the Treatise called Secrets Reveal'd: or An Open Entrance to the Shut-Palace of the King. That is to say, the Secret revealed, or the open entrance to the closed Palace of the King. Where the Author recounts the manner in which the Ancient Philosophers were able to discover their secret Work without Books.*

THE Ancient Philosophers believed that to achieve their design, besides an external heat, they also had need of an internal heat; this is why they set themselves to seek it in several things. First, they drew very hot waters from mineral means, with which they corroded the <term>Mercury</term> <note>In alchemy, Mercury often refers to the "spirit" or "soul" of matter rather than just the common metal quicksilver.</note>, but they could not by this means cause it to change its properties internally,

<summary>This page introduces translated excerpts from the alchemist Eirenaeus Philalethes regarding the search for a "universal solvent" and the internal heat required for the alchemical Great Work.</summary>
<keywords>Eirenaeus Philalethes, Alkaest, Secrets Revealed, Open Entrance to the Shut-Palace of the King, internal heat, alchemical mercury, ancient philosophers</keywords>

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Alkahest, specifically addressing its nature as a universal solvent that remains unchanged after its operation.</meta>

# The Alkahest or Solvent

Inasmuch as every corrosive water can only be an external Agent, little different from fire in its action, which does not remain with the body it has dissolved: Confirming by the same reasoning, they rejected all sorts of Salts, with one single exception, which is the first being <note>The "first being" or "ens primum" refers to the primordial state of a substance in alchemical theory</note> of salts, which dissolves all sorts of Metals, and by the same means coagulates Mercury, but through a violent way. This is why this Agent can be entirely separated from it, without losing anything of its weight or its virtues.

### II.

### *From the Commentary on the Epistle of Ripley to King Edward. Second conclusion.*

<margin>note</margin>
This sulfur <note>original: "soulphre"; in this context, it refers to the active, combustible principle within a substance rather than common brimstone</note> is not even lacking in common Mercury; it is also by its means that it can be precipitated into the form of a dry powder. This **sulfur** <note>In alchemy, sulfur represents the principle of combustibility and color, not just the chemical element.</note> is pure in gold and in silver, and less pure in the other metals, because it is fixed in the former and volatile in the latter. It is coagulated in all of them, and capable of being coagulated in **quicksilver** <note>mercury</note>. This sulfur is so strongly united in gold, in silver, and in mercury, that the **Ancients** <note>Refers to classical and medieval alchemists</note> always believed that sulfur and quicksilver were one and the same thing.

As for us, by means of the **Liquor** <note>Likely referring to the 'Alkahest' or universal solvent</note> of which we have just spoken—the invention of which we owe, in the part of the world we inhabit, to **Paracelsus** <note>A seminal 16th-century Swiss physician and alchemist</note>, even though it was and still is common among the **Moors** and the **Arabs**, and among some of the most ingenious chemists—by means of this Liquor, I say, we know that the sulfur, which is coagulable in mercury and coagulated in the other metals, is external to the internal nature of mercury. We know that it can be separated from it in the form of a tinted and metallic oil; the mercury remains stripped of all sulfur, except for that which one might call its **internal or central sulfur**, which can only be coagulated by our

One way, therefore, of reducing Metals and minerals into flowing Mercury <note>original: "Mercure coulant," referring to liquid quicksilver</note> is by means of the Alkaest Liquor, which from all bodies composed of Mercury can separate a flowing Mercury, or quicksilver, from which all the sulfur is then separated, except for its internal and central sulfur which no corrosive can touch. Besides this universal way of reduction, there are other particular ways by which one can reduce Saturn <note>Lead</note>, Jupiter <note>Tin</note>, and Antimony, even Venus <note>Copper</note> and Mars <note>Iron</note> into flowing Mercury; and this by means of Salts. ### III.

## From the Commentary on the Preface of the Twelve Gates by Ripley. Toward the end.

Some propose to extract from pure gold the Medicine called Potable Gold <note>Latin: Aurum Potabile; a legendary liquid medicine made from gold, believed to grant longevity</note>, by causing the gold to be eroded by *aqua regia* <note>A highly corrosive mixture of nitric and sulfuric acids, literally "royal water"</note>, and subsequently rendering it more subtle through repeated calcinations <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder</term> in the fire, and by manual triturations <term>trituration: the act of grinding or rubbing a solid into a fine powder</term>. They strive after that, by means of Liquors which they call Menstruums <term>menstruum: a solvent used to extract or dissolve materials</term>, to dissolve the lime <note>calx: the powdery metallic residue left after heating</note> thus subtilized: but all this is quite uselessly done, for there is only one single Menstruum which has the power to resolve gold, and the other sublunary bodies, into their first matter. Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a famous Swiss physician and alchemist</note>, the first Author of this Menstruum or dissolving water, calls it his **Alkaest**, his **fire of Gehenna** <note>original: "feu de Gehenne"; a reference to an unquenchable, spiritual fire</note>, his **corrosive specific**, and gives it several other names besides. The Medicine drawn from gold and prepared by this Menstruum or Alkaest, being real and Philosophical, is without doubt an excellent Medicine, but known only to the Adepts <note>those who have mastered the "Great Work" of alchemy</note>. However, it is not our "Great Medicine"; for being only a resolution of gold taken in its unique simplicity, it does

# V.

## Concerning the Commentary on the fourth gate of Ripley.

But for Philosophical agreement, I would more willingly follow the sentiment of the Illustrious Philosopher of Brussels (this is Van-Helmont <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644), a chemist and physician from the Spanish Netherlands who bridged the gap between alchemy and early chemistry.</note>,) whose Writings, as I estimate, will be judged to contain the most profound discoveries of Philosophy that have yet appeared, when they are published as we have been promised. I admire him less for his experiments, none of which are unknown to me, and for which he owes the discovery of the greatest part to Paracelsus <note>The famous Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of minerals in medicine.</note>: several of which are much more difficult to work than our <term>Elixir</term> <note>The Elixir of Life or the Philosopher's Stone in liquid form.</note>, although they are sooner completed, such as the <term>Alkahest</term> <note>A hypothetical universal solvent capable of dissolving every other substance, including gold.</note> which is a hundred times more difficult: I admire less, I say, this rare Naturalist for his experiments than for his Researches into the most hidden things of Nature, which are incontestably the most exact that one

has yet made. So much so that, if one excepts the Secret of the **Great Elixir** <note>The Philosopher's Stone, capable of transmuting base metals into gold and granting longevity.</note>, of which I have not yet been able to perceive any trace in his writings, one can say without flattery that he belongs to the **Privy Council of Nature**, and that he is ignorant of none of her secrets. Furthermore, for the sake of **Philosophical Truth**, he could have contributed much to the esteem of this Great Secret had he been its possessor; but God does not reveal all things to all men, and we do not know if some day he might yet possess this knowledge as well as the others he already has.

I do not say this to flatter him; one can form an idea of him from his writings similar to the one I have just sketched. I simply note the character that I have formed of his mind, as nothing obliges me to pretend, since I am unknown to him, and perhaps he will never know me. It is true that there is no one in the world whose acquaintance I would value as much as his; therefore, if his death or mine does not prevent my designs, I shall strive to win his friendship. Let this be said in passing.

* *This fire is the Mercury of the Philosophers.* <note>A metaphorical "fire" or solvent used in the Great Work, not literal flame.</note>

This is the power of this admirable Liquid over all kinds of matter.

93. All the Philosophers <note>A term used by alchemists to refer to their predecessors in the "Hermetic Art"</note> agree that our Mercury <term>Philosophical Mercury: not the common liquid metal, but a refined spiritual substance used in the Great Work</term> is nothing other than a Mercury that wets only what is homogeneous to metal, and which is the mother of the Stone <note>The Philosopher's Stone</note>. # The Alkaest or Solvent

simple & immortal, which penetrates all things & resolves them into their first liquid matter: nothing can resist its virtue: it acts without reaction from the thing upon which it acts, & suffers only from its like, which alone brings it under the yoke. After it has dissolved all other things, it remains entirely in its first nature, & has no less virtue after having served a thousand times than it had in its first action. <note>The Alkaest was a hypothetical "universal solvent" sought by alchemists, capable of reducing any body to its primary liquid essence without being consumed in the process.</note>

**Q.** What is its substance?

**A.** Its substance is an excellent **circulated Salt**, prepared in an admirable manner until it responds to the desires of a subtle **Artist**. <note>In alchemical texts, "Artist" refers to the practicing alchemist or chemist.</note> For one must not imagine that it is a corporeal Salt, as it is, rendered liquid by a simple dissolution: but rather a saline spirit that heat cannot thicken by the evaporation of its moisture: its substance being spiritual, uniform, volatile at a low heat, & leaving nothing behind after its evaporation. It is neither an acidic nor an alkaline spirit, but a saline spirit. <note>The author distinguishes this "saline spirit" from common acids or bases, emphasizing its purity and its ability to vaporize completely without leaving residue.</note>

**Q.** What do you call its like?

**A.** If you knew one of these two things, the other would not be unknown to you for long. Seek, for the Gods do not give—

# Van Helmont Revealed

...ment the Arts, only as a reward for industry <note>The author suggests that alchemical secrets are granted by God only to those who work hard at their craft</note>.

**Question:** What is the immediate matter of the **Alkahest** <term>Alkahest: a mythical universal solvent capable of dissolving any substance into its primary liquid form</term>?

**Response:** I have told you that it is a salt. Fire surrounds the Salt, and water swallows the fire without extinguishing it: and in this manner is made the fire of the Philosophers, of which it has been said, *the common people burn by fire, we by water* <note>original: "vulgus cremat per ignem, nos per aquam." This refers to "wet" chemistry or corrosive dissolution rather than dry heat</note>.

**Question:** Which is the most excellent of Salts?

**Response:** Do you wish to learn it? Descend into yourselves, and if you are capable of discernment, you will recognize there this Salt and its **Vulcan** <note>Vulcan: the Roman god of fire, used here to represent the internal transformative heat or energy</note> which you carry everywhere with you.

**Question:** Tell me, I pray you, what do you mean by that?

**Response:** I mean the blood drawn from the human body, or human Urine. For Urine is an excrement separated from the blood, for the most part. Both give a **volatile Salt** and a **fixed Salt**: if you knew how to extract and prepare them, you would have a very precious **Balm of Life**.

**Question:** Does the Urine of men have more virtue than that of other Animals?

**Response:** It has infinitely more. For although the Urine of men is but an excrement, its Salt nevertheless has no equal in all of nature.

# The Alkaest or Solvent

**Q.** What are the components of Urine?
**A.** Urine has volatile parts and fixed parts, and both are altered differently depending on the manner in which they are treated.

**Q.** Is there anything found in Urine that differs from its intimate and specific urinous nature?
**A.** Undoubtedly, for one finds there aqueous phlegm <note>In pre-modern medicine, "phlegm" refers to a watery, tasteless substance or one of the four humors</note> and the sea salt that we take in our food; this latter, remaining whole and undigested in the Urine, can be separated from it. If we go some time without taking enough of it in our meals, it will cease to be found in our Urine.

**Q.** From where does this phlegm, or aqueous and tasteless moisture found in Urine, proceed?
**A.** It proceeds principally from the liquids we drink, in addition to the fact that everything we eat also has its own phlegm.

**Q.** Explain yourself more clearly?
**A.** Know that Urine consists partly of that which is conducted into the bladder by the separating power <note>The "separative virtue" or power was thought to be a natural faculty of the body that sorted nutrients from waste</note>, together with what we drink, and partly of aqueous *Lefas* <note>original: "Lefas"; a Paracelsian term referring to the watery waste or excrement of the blood</note>, or moist excrement separated from the mass of the blood by the odor of the urinous Ferment—

# Van Helmont Revealed

**Question:** What are these qualities?

**Answer:** Yes, undoubtedly, and in every kind of Urine—even in that of the healthiest man—and it can be prepared by Art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy or early Chemistry</note>.

**Question:** How powerful is this Spirit?
**Answer:** Its power is such that it is formidable to men; those who feel its effects are much to be pitied.

**Question:** Why?
**Answer:** Because the *Duelech*, our most cruel enemy, draws its origin from it.

**Question:** Would you be willing to give us some example of the production of this Spirit?

### CHAPTER IX.

## CONCERNING THE ALKAHEST.

We have now come to the contemplation of a subject of miracles, for the Alkahest <term>Alkahest: a hypothetical universal solvent capable of reducing any substance to its primary liquid form without losing its own potency</term> is, without doubt, one of the most admirable Secrets of Nature. It is an immortal and incorruptible being, which can reduce all mixed bodies into their first liquid matter, destroying their corporeal solidity and volatilizing them.

The German Name, which Paracelsus <note>The influential Swiss-German physician and alchemist (1493–1541) credited with first using the term Alkahest.</note> gave to it

<summary>This page introduces a translated treatise by George Starkey regarding the "Alkahest," a legendary universal solvent sought by alchemists to reduce matter to its primary state.</summary>
<keywords>Alkahest, George Starkey, Van Helmont, Paracelsus, universal solvent, Pyrotechny, alchemy, immortality of matter</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of chemical principles, specifically focusing on the universal solvent known as the Alkahest, as described by the Flemish physician Jan Baptist van Helmont.</meta>

# Van Helmont Revealed

The name first given to it, composed of the two words *Al-gehest* <note>original: "Al-gehest", likely a pseudo-Arabic construction used by Paracelsus and Van Helmont to imply "all-spirit"</note>, which signify "All Spirit," can sufficiently indicate its nature. It is indeed a spirit of a substance so homogeneous that it cannot be altered in its nature except by its like (its companion), which changes it and causes it to lose its power when they are joined and mixed together.

I do not intend to make a long discourse here on a subject that I have already treated sufficiently and in a clear enough manner in a book dedicated to it; nor will I repeat what I have already said elsewhere. But since my goal in this work is to provide an abbreviated system of the entire art of Pyrotechnics <term>Pyrotechnics: here referring to the art of chemistry or "fire-working," rather than modern fireworks</term>, I cannot dispense with speaking of a liquid with which the most excellent preparations are perfected, unless I wished for this work to be considered imperfect.

Let us say, then, that the Alkahest is nothing other than that fire of which it has been said: "the common people burn with fire, and we burn with water." <note>original Latin: "Vulgus igne cremat nos aqua"</note> This is the fire that the illustrious Van Helmont calls his supreme and perpetual corrosive, his "fire of Hell" <note>original Latin: "Summum & perpetuum corrosivum... Gehennæ ignis"</note>. And of which we shall speak here only of its effects, its matter, and its pre-

<meta>catchword: pre- (preparation)</meta>

<keywords>Alkahest, Van Helmont, Pyrotechnics, universal solvent, Al-gehest, alchemy, corrosive</keywords>

# CHAPTER X.

## On the virtue and efficacy of the Alkahest in general.

The good and the useful being reciprocal, a thing cannot be called good unless it can also be called useful. This is why it will suffice for me to speak here of the utility of our Liquor <note>The "Liquor" refers to the Alkahest, the fabled universal solvent</note>, to draw men to seek it.
    It seems to me that I could not better begin the discourse on the advantages of this rare Secret than with the words of the Illustrious Van Helmont <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644), a foundational chemist and physician who popularized the term 'Alkahest'</note>; there is, he says, but one fire in the World, which is our burning Vulcan <note>original: "Vulcan"; the Roman god of fire, used here to represent the transformative power of heat and chemical reaction</note>. ## CHAPTER XI.

### *On the virtue or efficacy of the Alkahest in particular.*

IT will perhaps be believed that it is not my intention to distinguish the Alkahest <note>The "Alkahest" was a hypothetical universal solvent sought by alchemists, capable of reducing any body to its primary matter.</note> from other Solvents <note>original: "Dissolvans"</note>, which seem to have an affinity and relationship with it: but one will judge otherwise if one considers the errors that this mistake causes in those who devote themselves to its search; leading them to imagine uncertain materials upon which they apply their labor. This results in them proceeding impertinently, straying from the end they had proposed for themselves.

Among those, we will first consider those who make no difference between the Alkahest and the Mercury of the Philosophers <note>The "Mercury of the Philosophers" is a technical term for a refined, spiritualized form of mercury believed to be the "seed" of metals, distinct from common quicksilver.</note>. I know several who do not even wish to be disabused of this error, even though one finds nothing more absurd when it is put to the examination of Reason.

<summary>This chapter begins an investigation into the specific powers of the Alkahest, specifically warning against the common alchemical error of confusing it with the "Mercury of the Philosophers."</summary>
<keywords>Alkahest, universal solvent, Mercury of the Philosophers, alchemical theory, chemical methodology</keywords>

--- Page 155 ---
For these two things differ from one another materially and substantially: the one being properly called Mercury, because it is so in effect; and the other being a true Salt, is rightly called Circulated Salt, or the Great Circulate <note>The "Circulatus" refers to a substance purified through repeated distillation or "circulation" in a closed vessel</note>; the supreme and most excellent Salt, and the Liquor of Salt.

They also differ formally and essentially; the Mercury of the Philosophers being not only a metallic thing, but a true metal; that is to say, a Philosophical metal: the Philosopher having declared that within Metals, Metals are perfected by Metals. And by the common consent of all the Masters of the Art, this conclusion has been formed; Namely: That all the principles of the Elixir of the Philosophers are homogeneous; that they are co-essential to one another, and because of this they remain formally with one another; and they are changed into the nature of one another; the Agents becoming patients, and the patients Agents <note>In alchemy, the "agent" is the active force and the "patient" is the matter being acted upon</note>, in the progress of this incomparable Medicine. And it is for this very reason that this water of the Philosophers is called dry water, which wets neither the hands nor other things that are not of the same nature or the same matter as itself; *Dry water that does not wet the hands, nor anything* <note>original: "Aqua sicca non madefaciens manus, nec quic-"</note>

# The Alkaest or Solvent

...themselves, as they appear in their form, in their matter, and in their action.

The **Mercury of the Philosophers** is a Saturnine antimonial quicksilver <note>A liquid metal derived from antimony and lead (Saturn), considered a primary building block in alchemy</note>, a middle substance, shining like pure silver, according to the account of Artephius <note>A legendary alchemical author, possibly 12th-century, often cited as an authority on the longevity of life</note>. And the **Alkaest Liquor** is a Salt of a fiery nature, which has no equal in the World; which is neither mineral nor metallic; but which has been circulated <note>Refined through repeated distillation</note> until it becomes a pure Spirit. This is why it is called in German *Al. gehest* <note>original: "Al. gehest"; likely a wordplay on "All-Geist" meaning "Universal Spirit" or "All-goes," referring to its power to dissolve everything</note>.

The **Mercury of the Philosophers** does not wet the hands, nor any other thing that is not of its nature; that is to say, which is not metallic: and it unites with nothing except that which is metallic. The **Alkaest** wets the hands like any other thing. It dissolves all compounds according to their species, and reduces them into their first matter. It mixes with the parts of their dissolution in the same manner that a spirit mixes with its phlegm <note>The watery, inactive byproduct of distillation</note>. But not being radically joined with them, it can be separated from them.

In the **Mercury of the Philosophers**, gold alone sinks into it, is submerged in it, and is dissolved in it: the solvent and the thing dissolved remain united in an inseparable union, so that from the two, only one and the same thing is made. In the **Alkaest Liquor**...

# Van Helmont Revealed

On the contrary, not only gold, but every other metal sinks into it and dissolves within it; yet the Liquid does not remain with any of them, nor does it lose any of its strength by dissolving them.

Finally, the dissolution performed by the **Mercury of the Philosophers** <note>A specialized alchemical solvent, distinct from common quicksilver, believed to be the "root" of metals</note> is a kind of generation; the tincture or sulfur <note>In alchemy, "sulfur" refers to the active, combustible, or "soul" principle of a substance, not just the chemical element</note> does not separate from the Mercurial substance in this operation. On the contrary, it unites with it more strongly, so that the Solvent itself and the dissolved thing become a substance capable of multiplication within their own kind. 

But the dissolution performed by the **Alkahest** <note>The universal solvent theorized by Paracelsus and championed by Van Helmont</note> is a destructive dissolution, which extinguishes the energy of the seed and renders it powerless for generation. For the Alkahest separates the tincture from the Mercurial substance of metallic matters; so that once these two things are disunited, they can never be rejoined. It is true that by making this tincture volatile, the Alkahest renders it admirable for **Medicine**, but entirely removed from the metallic nature and the disposition it previously had for **Metals**.

To conclude all these differences, we shall add that although the Mercury of the Philosophers and the Alkahest are excellent Secrets, they are nevertheless so distinct

There are still some people who think that this Liquid is a Mercurial water; the author of the <term>Chemical Dictionary</term> <note>Likely referring to the work of William Johnson or a similar 17th-century compendium</note> is among this number, saying that the <term>Alkahest</term> <note>The "universal solvent" sought by alchemists, capable of reducing any body to its primary liquid state</note> is <term>Mercury</term> very well prepared against obstructions of the Liver. There are others who estimate that <term>Vitriol</term> <note>Sulfate minerals, often used to produce sulfuric acid</note> is its base material. That is to say, they believe that the <term>Spirit</term> of this mineral must be circulated with <term>Spirit of wine</term> <note>Ethanol or highly distilled alcohol</note> to become the <term>Alkahest</term>. Finally, others maintain that it is nothing but a pure <term>spirit of Salt</term> <note>Hydrochloric acid</note>. The Dreamers are divided into two opinions on this matter. Some want it to be a spiritual, Ethereal water, drawn from the Air, imprinted with an <term>esurine Salt</term> <note>A corrosive or "hungry" acid salt believed to exist in the atmosphere</note>; and others that it is the <term>Spirit of true Nitre</term>, which they distinguish from ordinary <term>Saltpeter</term>. But we shall leave both of these latter groups to seek their material, for I doubt that they themselves know where to find it, far from being able to teach it to others.

As for me, I shall let each man abound in his own sense, without troubling myself with what the opinions of others are on this subject.

# Van Helmont Revealed

I will say only this: my own experiences have taught me all too well that the most ingenious subtleties in theory or speculation are, more often than not, found to be nothing but pure daydreams when put into practice.

Van Helmont states positively that, just as there is only one fire in the world, there is likewise only one single Liquid which possesses the qualities of the one we are discussing—as the Adepts <note>A term for those who have mastered the "Great Work" of alchemy.</note> know and can testify. The words of this great man deserve my belief, and I owe it to them as a disciple owes it to his master. But to speak frankly, even though I find no reason that convinces my mind of their truth, I am nonetheless certain that I know the preparation of the Liquid he describes.

He further asserts in the 9th chapter of his treatise *On Lithiasis* <note>original: "Lithiasi"; a medical work by Van Helmont concerning the formation of stones in the body.</note> that the preparation of the Alkahest <note>The hypothetical universal solvent of the alchemists.</note> is extremely tedious. # The Alkaest or Solvent

The length and the difficulty of the preparation of the Alkaest <note>The "Alkaest" was a hypothetical universal solvent sought by alchemists, capable of reducing any body to its primary matter.</note> is unknown to me, although I am certain, as I have already said, that I know the preparation of a Liquid which produces the effects that this great Philosopher attributes to his own. But whether his and mine are the same, or are similar in all things, I would dare neither to affirm nor to deny. However, I hope to be able to prepare the one I know in 50 days, and even if I said in 40 days, I do not believe I would be mistaken.

The first time that I prepared this Liquid, as I was working without certainty or by guesswork, I often made mistakes. Thus, I am persuaded that during this work, I took the longest path that one could possibly take to prepare it. Furthermore, having foreseen that I might make several mistakes, I initially worked on a great deal of matter <note>original: "matiere"; refers to the raw chemical substances used in the experiment.</note> at once, so that if two or three trials happened to fail, I might still have enough to begin others again.

Having achieved my design, I examine my other works; and provided I am not mistaken in my calculations, it is certain that I know several chemical operations <term>Chymiques<note>The archaic spelling of "Chemical," referring here to the art of alchemy and early laboratory science.</note></term> much longer than that one. Thus, I do not see that strong proof which the <term>Adepts<note>A term for those who have mastered the secrets of alchemy, particularly the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note></term> claim regarding this tedious preparation—unless Van Helmont took this "tedium" to mean not the length of time, but the discomfort caused by the subject upon which one works during its first preparations; and this is what I would more readily believe. Furthermore, this discomfort may be greater for one artist than for another, depending on the path he takes, the method he follows, or the instruments he uses. For a very great variety can be encountered in these sorts of things, even though they all tend toward the same goal. Let that be said only in passing; let us resume the course of our design.

The Liquor of which we speak is a heavy Liquor, being nothing other than Salt without <term>phlegm<note>In early chemistry, "phlegm" referred to the watery, tasteless, and odorless part of a substance obtained through distillation.</note></term>. It is entirely volatile, because it is all Spirit, separated from every coarse excrement <note>original: "excrément groſſier," referring to the dross or impure solid remains of a substance.</note>. Its odor is weak, insofar as everything that has a strong odor is for the most part either volatile or composed of several heterogeneous parts. Now

# The Alkaest or Solvent

This Liquor, although volatile, is nevertheless not so to the same degree as <term>Spirit of Wine</term> <note>Alcohol or ethanol</note>, <term>Spirit of Urine</term> <note>Ammonia solution</note>, or any other similar Spirit which flies away at the slightest heat. Rather, it is volatile to the degree of heavy Spirits, which surrender their phlegm <note>The watery, non-active part of a liquid</note> during distillation before rising. Furthermore, after it has dissolved vegetable matter and rendered them volatile, it allows them to evaporate entirely and separate from it at the relatively weak heat of a <term>Bain-Marie</term> <note>A water bath used for gentle, uniform heating</note>. It allows them, I say, to rise alone, adorned with their different colors; and they, in contrast, leave this Liquor—which dissolved and volatilized them—at the bottom of the <term>cucurbit</term> <note>The lower, gourd-shaped vessel of a distillation apparatus</note>, in the same quantity and with just as much virtue <note>Potency or medicinal power</note> as it possessed before it had dissolved them.

Finally, this Liquor is an immortal being; I mean to say that it is a substance whose virtue is never exhausted by the continuity of its action upon mixed bodies. Instead, it preserves its vigor without alteration, being always ready to dissolve bodies. It is subject only to accidents <note>External physical changes</note>, but not to a change of its nature, unless it be by means of its own kind. And it is because of all these beautiful qualities that those who know it esteem it a Secret without equal.

<summary>This page describes the unique distillation properties of the Alkaest, noting that it remains unchanged and reusable after separating from the substances it has dissolved. The author emphasizes the solvent's "immortality," as its chemical potency is never depleted through use.</summary>

### CHAPTER XII.

#### *On the Remedies that can be prepared by the Alkaest.*

From the things already reported concerning the miraculous nature of the Alkaest <note>The "Alkaest" was a hypothetical universal solvent sought by alchemists, famously described by Jan Baptist van Helmont.</note>, one can easily understand how useful this rare solvent would be in the hands of a learned and judicious Artist <note>In this context, "Artist" refers to a skilled practitioner of alchemy or chemistry.</note>, for the perfection of Medicine and Physics <note>original: "Physique" - here meaning natural philosophy or the study of nature.</note>. For without seeking other proofs, are those of the admirable Medicinal virtues found in Metals, in Minerals, in precious Stones, in Pearls, and in the stones of Animals and Vegetables not convincing enough? Since it is by this Liquor that they are developed from all these materials to prepare admirable Remedies.

The resolution <note>The process of breaking a substance down into its component parts.</note> of all vegetables by this same Liquor is no less urgent: for it resolves them into their first liquid matter, distinguishing all their heterogeneous parts by their different colors and by the positions they take—

# The *Alkahest* or *Solvent*

Until now, I have not encountered the opportunity to repeat these experiments, having contented myself with the thought that if God finds me capable of serving my neighbor through these kinds of things, He will provide me with the means at the same time. If He ordains otherwise, blessed be His Name. He had given me Talents of which perhaps He found me unworthy; thus, He has rendered me incapable of helping others with them or profiting from them myself.

I have seen several effects of this *Liquor* <note>The "Liquor" refers to the Alkahest, the theorized universal solvent of the alchemists.</note>, and I know of others approaching it which my Readings and Meditation confirm. So that I know that what I write is true, that I have the experience of it, and that I have seen it with my own eyes: the most convincing proof that we can have on this earth.

# CHAPTER XIII.
## *Of the matter of the Alkaest, and of the manner of preparing it.*

The surprising effects of this Liquor, and the inexpressible wonders that one can perform with it when one possesses it, have engaged several Artists <note>In this context, "Artists" refers to practitioners of the "Hermetic Art" or alchemy.</note> to seek it out, and not without reason, since the possession of it abundantly rewards the pains and the expenses employed therein.

But it happens in this search,

# The Alkaest or Solvent

As in any other pursuit, unless one seeks within the proper materials and works them in the manner they require, our efforts are useless. *In the proper matter, through the proper means.* <note>original: "In debita materia, per debita media." This is a common alchemical axiom emphasizing that the result depends entirely on the starting substance and the correct process.</note>

Anything new or surprising that one might produce—no matter how beautiful its qualities may be once finished—will not convince us, by that alone, that it is the <term>Liquor</term> <note>A term often used interchangeably with the Alkaest or the universal solvent.</note> of which we speak. Let the Artist work as much as he likes according to his own fancy; Nature will not change her rules for that, nor will she transgress her own laws to execute the daydreams of that Artist. Instead, she will only do what she is obliged to do, according to the orders she has received from her Creator.

This is why we shall exclude from this masterpiece all metals and all metallic substances. For as for their <term>central Mercury</term> <note>In alchemical theory, this refers to the inner, essential spirit or "seed" of a metal that remains unchanged by external chemical reactions.</note>, being without equal, far from mixing with anything, it remains alone and unalterable. Furthermore, being true Mercury, it wets only that which is of its own kind; that is to say, that which is mercurial like itself. Thus, it cannot be the Liquor we seek of its own accord, much less can it become so by Art, since it cannot be mixed with anything, neither by <term>sublimation</term> <note>The process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then cools back into a solid, used for purification.</note> nor by <term>dissolution</term> <note>The process of dissolving a solid into a liquid state.</note>.

<summary>The author argues that the Alkaest cannot be derived from metals or metallic mercury because their internal nature is immutable and refuses to bond with foreign substances. He emphasizes that alchemical success requires following the fixed laws of Nature rather than the artist's imagination.</summary>

# Van Helmont Revealed

Regarding their **Sulphur** <note>In alchemy, sulphur represents the principle of combustibility and soul, not just the chemical element.</note>, since it cannot be radically separated from their **Mercury** <note>The principle of fluidity and spirit.</note> except by means of this **Liquor** <note>Referring to the Alkahest, the fabled universal solvent.</note>; it would be a great simplicity to believe that this sulphur could be the raw material for it. Indeed, it would follow the absurdity that one would need to have this Liquor already completed before one could obtain the material from which it must be made.

Likewise, we exclude the combustible Sulphurs of **Minerals**, because being sluggish bodies without action, they cannot be truly altered in their nature. And it is for this reason that one can certainly make **passive Remedies** from them, but not **active Menstruums** <note>term: Menstruum (a solvent used to extract virtues from solids)</note>. And although these passive remedies act strongly enough regarding Diseases, they nevertheless lack action toward mixed bodies, because they do not have the power to dissolve, unless they are burned; for then they yield an **acid Liquor**, which is truly active.

It is therefore for this reason that we do not take as the material for the **Alkahest** <note>The "universal solvent" sought by Paracelsus and Van Helmont.</note> either the mercuries or the metallic Sulphurs, any more than the Sulphurs of Minerals. **Metallic Salts** are also excluded, because without exception, they all yield an **acid Spirit** <note>A volatile acidic vapor or liquid.</note> which is contrary to the nature of our—

Solvent. For if this Liquid were acidic, it would not be immutable in its action, as it must be according to that infallible Rule of <term>Chemical Philosophy</term> <note>The theoretical framework of alchemy and early chemistry</note>, which dictates that every acidic Spirit that corrodes a body weakens itself: *Every acidic spirit, by corroding a body, is itself exhausted.* <note>original: "Omnis acidus spiritus corrodendo corpus ipse satifcit."</note>

This last reason also makes us reject as useless for its material: Saltpeter, Vitriol, Rock Salt, Common Salt, and all the other Salts that are born naturally in the earth, or that are drawn from the earth, because they all yield an acidic Spirit.

The <term>Alkalis</term> <note>Basic substances, the opposite of acids</note> could claim, with justice, preeminence over all the Salts we have named: for their Spirits, not being at all acidic, are undoubtedly significant Solvents, and very close to our Liquid: we shall therefore speak of them at great length in the following section. However, these Spirits, though excellent, lose their virtue by dissolving bodies and by coagulating upon them into a Salt that retains its volatility; they cannot, consequently, be the subject of our Liquid.

# The Alkahest or Solvent

A body had been necessary for the matter of his <term>Alkahest</term><note>The "Alkahest" was a hypothetical universal solvent sought by alchemists, capable of reducing any body to its primary liquid matter.</note>, as some have believed, who insist that one must take Mercury and Tartar; and even several other materials to make it. But what could one expect from this impertinent mixture, if not a languid Liquor, if it were not entirely without virtue; and therefore unfit for any considerable action.

It is therefore a single Body and not multiple Bodies that the Art desires with such eagerness: but a Body which, being radically one in essence, shows to the sight a very distinct double diversity, but only in qualities: and which fundamentally agree in such a way that, being touched by the skillful hand of an Artist, they can cause a harmonious melody to his ear.

One could say of this Body—unique in essence or in genus, and double in number or appearance—what <term>Hermes</term><note>Refers to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the foundational texts of alchemy are attributed.</note> on another occasion said of the Mercury of the Philosophers and its Peer: namely, *That what is below is like what is above, and what is above is like what is below, to produce the miracles of one thing.* <note>original: "Que ce qui est en bas, est comme ce qui est en haut..." This is the famous opening of the Emerald Tablet, the foundational text of Hermetic philosophy.</note>

# Van Helmont Revealed.

Regarding the matter of this excellent Liquor: imprint therefore upon your mind that only one Body is required in kind and reality, yet distinct under two appearances, which are superficially different. This Body is not found nor obtained easily, since the words of Van Helmont bear such visible testimony to the difficulties involved when he says: that Art seeks out a Body with care, with industry, and with application. For one must observe that the word *Indagando* <note>Latin: "by searching out" or "tracking."</note>, which signifies the search for this Body, implies a careful, studious, exact, and continual search, like that of hunting dogs who ferret out and follow a beast by the scent—the odor of the feet that the animal leaves upon the earth while fleeing—keeping them always in pursuit. This word, composed of *inde* <note>from there</note> and *ago* <note>I act/drive</note>, signifies a continuous and relentless action based on certain known principles, until one has obtained what is claimed. And this is our second discovery touching upon the matter of our Liquor, of which you must carefully preserve the memory.

# The Alkaest or Solvent

It brings surprise and astonishment to the Artist <note>In alchemical texts, "the Artist" refers to the practicing alchemist or chemist.</note> to be unable to comprehend how such a Body could be a subject, like the one in which it is found. This is why our subtle Philosopher adds: *Finally, Religion was struck with amazement upon the discovery of the Liquid, etc.* <note>original: "Tandem stupefacta est Religio, reperto Latice, &c." This likely refers to the spiritual awe felt when witnessing the "Latex" or primordial water of the philosophers.</note> In the end, this production puts the Artist into such a state of wonder that, full of veneration and gratitude at seeing what he has found, he is forced to cry out, *Lord, how marvelous You are in Your Works.*

The thing being found, and the discovery of it being made, one can certainly say that it is the Work of God, and not the work of men. Who can, says Job, make a pure thing from an impure one? <note>A reference to Job 14:4.</note> There is no doubt that only God can do it.

One can find in this Subject mysteries astonishing enough to arrest our senses and surprise our reason. A dirty and repulsive matter yields a Body of the ultimate purity. A matter which is itself a Proteus <note>A Greek sea-god capable of changing his shape; here used to describe the volatile and shifting nature of raw matter.</note> in its changes and its continual alterations, produces a Being that is immutable and unalterable. To believe these wonders before one has seen them, does one not have need of a chemical Faith? Since, even upon seeing them, reason cannot consider them without astonishment.

# Van Helmont Revealed

This Mystery is little different from the Miracle of the Creation; where from a confused Abyss, so many different things, so admirable and so rare, were formed and produced. Or where from the bosom of a Chaos of darkness, there emerged all this glory, the excellent beauties, which rendered the Garden of Delights <note>The Garden of Eden</note> inestimable. If one wishes to reason in the same way regarding the unparalleled production of which we speak; the difference encountered between the thing produced and the subject that produces it is greater than can be imagined. It is therefore not without reason that the Art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy or the Great Work</note> finds itself so embarrassed to find a Body such as this one, in the thing where it seeks it. A Body, I say, which must be so pure; a Being so unalterable in its use; so active in its action; and so permanent in its virtue.

Let us now collect ourselves within, to see where we stand. We have found that the Subject <note>The "Subject" or "Prima Materia" is the raw starting material of the alchemical work</note> where this being is hidden, envelopes it and holds it so invisible and imperceptible under its foul appearances, that one would have to be, in some fashion, molded from credulity to believe in its existence there; yet, that one can extract it with industry and render it visible and apparent; and that it is then so different—

The fourth thing that falls under our observation in this discovery is that this Body, being singular, scorns to mix with anything by Fermentation. # Van Helmont Revealed

*Therefore its transmutation occurs, finding no body more worthy than itself to which it might be wedded.* <note>original Latin: "ideo eft ejus transmutatio, dignius se corpus non reperiens, cui nuberet."</note> And the operating or acting means by which it acquires this excellence, or particular preeminence, are the reduction of its parts into the smallest atoms that Nature can possibly make.

It is in this manner that this *Latex* <note>In Helmontian alchemy, "Latex" refers to a primordial water or a clear liquor, often considered vile or common in its crude state.</note>, vile and despicable, reaches this high degree of purity and perfection: which is easily said, but not so quickly understood, and which one could only achieve with even greater difficulty.

Paracelsus, in his treatise *On the Powers of the Limbs* <note>original Latin title: "De viribus membrorum"</note>, in the chapter *On the Liver* <note>original Latin: "de Hepate"</note>, teaches this operation in this way. The process of the *Alkahest* <term>Alkahest: a hypothetical universal solvent capable of dissolving every other substance into its primary matter</term> consists of dissolving it after its coagulation, and re-coagulating it after its dissolution, into a changed form, according to the manner taught by the method of coagulation and dissolution. *Its process is such that it is resolved from coagulation, and again coagulated into a transmuted form, just as the process of coagulating and resolving teaches.* <note>original Latin: "Ejus proceffus eft, ut à coagulatione refolvatur, & iterum coaguletur in formam transmutatam, ficut proceffus de coagulando & refolvendo docet."</note> This short preparation is the greatest light that this subtle philosopher has given us on this subject. This is why there is no reason to be surprised that the doctrine of it has remained so hidden until today.

For my part, having resolved to act with more sincerity than they have done, recognizing the utility that the publication of this Secret can bring to men, I shall not fear at all exposing myself to the censure of living Artists, nor incurring the blame of those who will follow us, for having discovered these Mysteries in a clearer and more intelligible manner than any other has ever done.

# Van Helmont Revealed

And to simultaneously bring order to what we have already said, let us consider in a few words the Doctrine of this great man concerning this Liquor.

Let us consider, I say, that it is a **Body of salt**, which appears under two forms, which can be reduced into such a **Consonance** or **Harmony** through its purity, that it is no longer, after that, subject to corruption. That this Body is found, by the curious and diligent research of Artists <note>In this context, "Artists" refers to alchemists or skilled chemical practitioners.</note>, in a matter that Van Helmont calls *Latex* <note>original: "Latex"; here referring to a primordial water or clear liquid principle in Van Helmont's system.</note>. That if one looks at the subject which hides it, one will remain surprised by the difference found in it before its preparation, and that which is encountered there when Art has finished it: being in its original matter a subject of contempt, and in its exaltation an object of admiration. Finally, that being perfect and finished, it no longer finds any Body that approaches its excellence to unite with it; and because it cannot mix with any thing by **Fermentation**, it cannot consequently be changed.

# The *Alkahest* or *Solvent*

Everything that has been reported concerning this Liquid can be suitably reduced to four Headings.

The first contains the goal of the Artist <note>The "Artist" refers to the alchemist or practitioner of the Hermetic arts.</note> in these words:
> The Chemical Art carefully seeks a Body which accords, or which has such a Consonance or Harmony with us because of its extreme purity, that no corrupting matter can dissipate it.
This is everything that the Artist proposes to obtain through his labor, and it is also the noblest goal one can propose in Chemistry.

The second marks what the Art must find, through industry, to arrive at this goal, and which is comprised in these words:
> But finally, the Artist having found a certain Liquid or *Latex* <note>original: "Latex" — here used in its classical sense meaning a pure fluid or liquid, often associated with water or primordial matter</note>, his astonishment becomes so great that it reaches the point of veneration. For this Liquid, being reduced to the smallest Atoms that can be produced by the aid of Nature, finds itself without equal, and scorns the union and the mixture of every kind of Ferment <note>A "Ferment" is a substance capable of causing a transformation or "ripening" in another body</note>: which renders its transmutation impossible, for it finds no Body more excellent than itself to which it might be united.
