# Van Helmont Revealed

production in these words: "So that the work of the Wise Chemists has formed an anomalous body, or one irregular in Nature, which was produced without the mixture of any ferment different from itself."

Finally, the fourth gives the summary or a brief description of the procedure of this Work in these words: "This serpent has bitten itself, and has taken up a new life from its own venom, so that it can no longer die." <note>This is a classic alchemical reference to the Ouroboros, symbolizing a self-refining process where a substance is purified by its own internal essence.</note>

This is how we have instructed ourselves in this rare Secret, and how we have discovered that the subject or the matter of what we seek is a Liquid or *Latex* <note>original: "Latex"; here referring to a primordial vegetable or mineral juice, a concept central to Van Helmont's "water-based" chemistry</note>; that in its intermediate production, it is a Body of two different Natures, between which there must finally arrive such a Consonance, agreement, or Symphony, that it becomes incorruptible. That in its final production or perfection, it is a subject incapable of Ferment <term>Ferment: a transformative agent or "yeast" that triggers chemical change</term>, and consequently incapable of transmutation. This must, however, be understood with certain kinds of limits.

Let us also consider what the Author adds. "So that," he says, "the work of the Wise Chemists has formed an anomalous or irregular Body in Nature." This Body was formed—

<summary>This page explains the final stages of an alchemical process where a substance becomes "anomalous" and incorruptible, using the metaphor of a self-healing serpent to describe its perfection.</summary>
<keywords>Van Helmont, alchemy, Wise Chemists, Ferment, Latex, incorruptibility, transmutation, Ouroboros</keywords>

--- Page 194 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Alkaest, focusing on its unique properties as a universal solvent that defies the standard laws of nature.</meta>

# The Alkaest or Solvent

born without the mixture of any heterogeneous Ferment <note>A substance of a different nature or origin.</note> or anything different from itself. It is a **Serpent** that has bitten itself, and which has drawn a new life from its own venom in order to make itself immortal.

The irregularity of this generation would require an entire treatise, if we wished to discover all its circumstances: but as this place does not permit me, I will content myself with saying only something of it in passing.

This **Body**, firstly, is anomalous or irregular in its Operations. For there is no Agent in the world that acts without a reaction, if one excepts the Celestial Bodies, to whom this property is natural, and among the Sublunary <note>Things existing beneath the moon; the earthly realm subject to change and decay.</note> things, **Fire**. However, this **Liquor** acts without receiving any alteration from the thing upon which it acts.

It is anomalous in its matter. Inasmuch as the Tree is ordinarily known by its fruits, and the matter by that which is produced from it: but here it happens quite differently: That which is produced is immortal, very pure, and incorruptible; even though the matter from which this production is drawn is the most corruptible thing in the world, the most impure, and the most changeable.

<summary>The author describes the Alkaest as a paradoxical substance that acts upon others without being changed itself, likening its purity to celestial bodies despite its origin in corruptible matter.</summary>
<keywords>Alkaest, universal solvent, alchemy, celestial bodies, sublunary realm, philosophical serpent, incorruptibility</keywords>

--- Page 195 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the philosophical stone or the "Great Work" as described in the tradition of Jan Baptist van Helmont, focusing on the final stages of its preparation and its symbolic nature.</meta>

# *Van Helmont Revealed*

It is also anomalous in the manner of its production: For it becomes a <term>Ferment</term> <note>In alchemy, a ferment is a substance that has the power to transform or "leaven" a larger mass into its own nature, much like yeast in bread.</note> unto itself, so that without any addition other than what comes from itself, this extraordinary Being is produced.

Finally, it remains for us to say a word regarding the means of its production. This Miracle of Art is achieved through repeated <term>dissolutions</term> <note>The process of breaking a solid down into a liquid state.</note> and by an intervening <term>coagulation</term> <note>The process of thickening or solidifying a liquid.</note>. And it is through this that its matter is reduced into <term>Atoms</term> as subtle as any that can be reduced within Nature.

This is the <term>Serpent</term> <note>original: "Serpent"; a reference to the Ouroboros, the ancient symbol of a snake eating its own tail, representing cyclicality and the unity of matter.</note> that bites and devours itself: this matter being in effect nothing but a Serpent, which like that reptile devours itself by degrees, beginning with its tail; and in the end, it is renewed into a pure <term>Essence</term> over which death no longer has any power.

I could say many things here regarding the truth of its mortality and its immortality, if I did not fear enlarging this Volume to excess. Furthermore, the design I initially proposed for myself—to speak of things only in Abridgment—and the promises I made to the Reader to entertain him with matters other than this one, do not permit me to do so. I pass therefore from this subject to the other things that remain for me to say.

<summary>This page describes the self-referential alchemical process of creating a "Ferment" through repeated dissolution and coagulation, using the symbol of the Ouroboros to illustrate the matter's transformation into an immortal essence.</summary>
<keywords>Van Helmont, Ferment, dissolution, coagulation, Atoms, Serpent, Ouroboros, Essence, Alchemy, mortality, immortality</keywords>

After a long debate within myself, I find myself finally obliged to bring to light this little Work, not only to do justice to the memory of a dead man, but also to show my inclination to gratify the living. For in an Age where <term>Natural Philosophy</term> <note>original: "la Physique"; in this context, it refers to the study of nature and alchemy rather than modern physics</note> triumphs through a considerable number of Artists <note>skilled practitioners or alchemists</note>, and of generous persons who protect them, and by the beautiful discoveries that have been made by their means: what could I do less than to communicate this Essay on the <term>Immortal Liquor</term>, or the <term>Alkahest</term> <note>A hypothetical universal solvent sought by alchemists, capable of dissolving every other substance</note>, since it is the key that is now sought with such care, and which puts us in possession of the rarest Secrets of Nature.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the universal solvent, specifically identifying its source and the philosophical nature of its preparation.</meta>

# Van Helmont Revealed

quantity nor of your virtue: you remain in the same number, same weight, and same measure, after having acted a thousand times upon them. There is only one thing that overcomes you, but it loses itself shamefully in your destruction. <note>The author is addressing the "Alkahest" or universal solvent, which remains unchanged by the substances it dissolves.</note>

This **Solvent** is both vile and precious; it costs nothing, and all men have it in their power, the poor as well as the rich. Adam carried it away with him when he departed from the Earthly Paradise. It is deeply hidden in the <term>Microcosm</term> <note>original: "petit Monde"; the human body as a reflection of the universe</note> and very powerful in the <term>Macrocosm</term> <note>original: "grand Monde"; the universe at large</note>. It overcomes and destroys all bodies, and reduces the most rebellious natures. Finally, it is the product of **human urine**: but as there is nothing easier to obtain, there is nothing more difficult to work with than this matter. This is what led Van Helmont to say that its preparation was very tedious, and that Wisdom will despise those who condemn a thing as vile and dirty as that, neglecting to instruct themselves, by the aid of **Fire**, regarding the things it contains.

I will say how I sought it, and in what manner after...

If in this account they find something worthy of imitation, they may follow my example, and perhaps, with God blessing their study, their labors, and their vigils, they may at last achieve their desires, just as I have arrived at the enjoyment of mine.

# Van Helmont Revealed

->*Lunar Blas*<- <note>Van Helmont coined the term "Blas" to describe a localized "astral" or "vital" force that governs the movement and changes of matter. The "Lunar Blas" specifically refers to the cooling, rhythmic influence associated with the Moon.</note>, to remain there invisible, even though it makes itself sufficiently recognized by its effects. So it is that this acid—which, through its action on other *Bodies*, receives different coagulations from their diversity in various forms of *Hard Salts*—receives its total destruction from this disguised *Body*, through its change into tasteless and Elementary water. The *Ammoniac Body* <note>original: "Corps Armoniac"</note> is thus protected from its blows by the *Blas* or Lunar influence. 

But as the weakening of the acid comes from the acid itself, which has exhausted itself by its own action and by its vain efforts against the disguised *Ammoniac Body*; the extinction of its Being, or of its saline life, and consequently of all its fury, must be entirely attributed to the *Lunar Blas*. This force is intimately and inseparably united to the form of the *Ammoniac*, whose coagulation into this disguised *Body* was brought about by the proper action of the *Urinous Spirit* upon itself, according to the infallible instinct that the *Wisdom of the Creator* has ordered it to follow.

# The Alkahest or Solvent

When one considers the extent of the power and energy of Urine with regard to its Spirit, it will be agreed that it cannot be admired enough. For there is nothing in the world—except for the center or nucleus of Mercury, and one thing which alone is its equal, the latter destroying it, and the former remaining unalterable by its action—there is nothing, I say, that it does not change, at least mediately, into its own nature, or that it does not absolutely destroy and reduce into purely Elemental water.

To demonstrate what I am putting forward, it will not be useless to examine exactly the effects of our fiery Liquor <note>original: "Liqueur ignée"; a term for the Alkahest, implying its caustic, fire-like potency</note> upon all sublunar mixtures <note>The "sublunar" realm refers to the Earthly sphere below the moon, subject to change and decay</note>. In the Mineral kingdom, except—as has already been said—the heart or nucleus of Mercury; all metallic and mineral sulfurs <note>In alchemy, "sulfur" refers to the combustible or soul-like principle of a substance</note>, even those of the Sun <note>Gold</note>, the Moon <note>Silver</note>, and of Mercury, are, by reiterated cohobations <note><term>Cohobation</term>: The process of repeatedly distilling a liquid off from the solid matter it was poured upon to increase its potency</note> with it, changed into Liquor, or saline Spirit, and at the end into tasteless and purely Elemental water.

In the same manner, all stones which cannot be calcined <note>Reduced to powder by heat</note> are changed into Salt by this infernal fire <note>A metaphorical name for the powerful solvent</note>, and this Salt, circulated and often cohobated together with this fire, becomes volatile, and by the addition of a certain thing, is changed at the end into water.

# THE ALKAHEST OR SOLVENT

<image-desc>A typographical headpiece ornament composed of four rows of repeating decorative fleur-de-lis and floral elements.</image-desc>

## REFLECTIONS ON THE
### *manner of making the Alkahest, which Starkey describes in the preceding Treatises.*

Although Starkey has hidden the Secret of the <term>Alkahest</term> <note>A hypothetical universal solvent sought by alchemists, capable of reducing any body to its primary matter.</note> within his Writings, as the Reader may have noticed while reading them: I nevertheless believe that he has said everything necessary to discover it. On this Foundation, I have examined these same Writings, and they have given me the opportunity to make the following discoveries. Whether my conjectures are fortunate or not, one may judge by reading these Reflections, while waiting for some Learned hand to decide the matter through experience.

I have therefore gathered three things from these Writings: the <term>remote matter</term> <note>The raw, unrefined starting substance.</note> of the Alkahest; the <term>proximate matter</term> <note>The substance once it has been prepared and is ready for the final chemical reaction.</note> from which it must be composed; and the manner in which one must guide this matter to form this great Solvent.

# Van Helmont Revealed

I have come to believe that the "remote matter" <note>The raw starting material in alchemy</note> must be human urine alone; that the "proximate matter" <note>The substance after its first processing</note> consisted of the three different Spirits that are drawn from this urine, according to Van Helmont; namely, a vinous or flammable Spirit, a urinous or burning Spirit, and a fermented Spirit which dissolves the *Duelec* <note>original: "Duelec"; a term used by Van Helmont to describe a type of stony calculus or tartar formed in the body</note> without corrosion. And I believe that the procedure for this entire Work was to extract these three Spirits from fermented human urine alone, to rectify them, to conjoin two of them into a condensed saline Body, and to dissolve this Body using the third Spirit through a Philosophical dissolution <note>A process where the solvent and the substance become one without losing their essential properties</note>, the Solvent and the dissolved thing remaining joined together and separated from everything heterogeneous to them.

Let us now see if all these things are found in our Author, and if my conjectures will be fortunate enough to convince the mind of the Reader as they have satisfied my own, and if they might merit the approval of some learned and judicious Artist <note>A practitioner of alchemy or chemistry</note>, to encourage them to put these ideas into practice.

# Van Helmont Revealed

Furthermore, to render what I have proposed beyond dispute, it remains to prove that these same Spirits of which Van Helmont spoke are the same ones intended by our Author <note>Likely George Starkey, an influential 17th-century alchemist who wrote under the pseudonym Eirenaeus Philalethes.</note>, and from which he forms his <term>Alkahest</term> <note>A hypothetical universal solvent capable of reducing any body into its primary liquid matter.</note>.

To better develop this Mystery, the Reader will not be discouraged if I report here the words of Starkey regarding these three Spirits: For it is principally his thought which is at issue here, and which we require, since we seek only the discovery of his Secret. Here, then, is what he says of it: *I add—these are his words—that this sharp, subtle, and penetrating Spirit of human Urine, by means of another Mediating Spirit, not of a different Ferment from its own, but centrally one with it, must be united to an Acid that is not corrosive, but very agreeable by its nature: This Acid must be as volatile as the Salt of Urine, before it can be intimately united with it.* These words therefore indicate to us three different Spirits; and these three Spirits, as I have proved, must be drawn from Urine alone.

The first of which our Author speaks here is the ordinary Spirit of Urine; that is to say, the <term>igneous and volatile spirit</term> <note>A reference to ammonium carbonate or ammonia, characterized by its "fiery" and evaporative nature in early chemistry.</note>; and this Spirit being one of those of which Van Helmont spoke, no proofs are necessary for this one—

Wine has so many similarities to urine that I believe it may have been the reason Starkey gave the name "Acid" to his Spirit, which he calls a non-corrosive Acid. # The Alkaest or Solvent

After the distillation of the burnt materials, one will obtain a fixed salt if they are leached. Similarly, once urine is stripped of its ardent spirit <note>Alcohol or flammable essence</note> and its volatile spirit or salt, and left to ferment for a suitable time, it will yield a spirit that one can call Acid, since it dissolves the <term>Duelech</term> <note>A Paracelsian term for a stone or calculus formed in the human body</note>. After this acid, it will yield a stinking oil and black, burnt materials, which will produce a fixed salt if they are calcined and leached. Such precise correlations between wine and urine undoubtedly led Starkey <note>George Starkey, an American alchemist and mentor to Robert Boyle</note> to name the third spirit drawn from urine "Acid," it being after all very similar to the acid spirit drawn from fermented wine when stripped of its ardent or flammable spirit. This acid spirit of wine holds preeminence among acids, since it is only in relation to it that all other acids are so named.

The second quality that Starkey attributes to his third spirit is that of being non-corrosive. This quality suits Van Helmont's third spirit so well that Van Helmont himself expressly says that it dissolves the <term>Duelech</term> without boiling and without agitation: *So that the Duelech is gradually diminished without bubbles or agitation* <note>original Latin: "Sic ut Duelech sensim minuatur absque bullis & agitatione." # Van Helmont Revealed

To speak of this third Spirit in this place—even if one did not wish to agree—Starkey himself could convince them of it. The third quality that Starkey gives to his third spirit is that it is "very agreeable to nature." Here are his words: *But it is most pleasing to its own nature* <note>original Latin: "Sed naturæ suæ gratissimum."</note>. This way of speaking is frequent in the writings of Van Helmont to denote some quality that does not cause harm, or which is suitable to some part of our body. In his treatise *Third Paradox, paragraph 10*, speaking of the esurine salt <note>original: "Sel esurin"; an acidic, "hungry" salt believed by Helmontians to be the primary digestive agent in the stomach.</note>, he says: *This salt, therefore, free from foreign admixture, is acidic, and in the proper quantity is pleasing to our body* <note>original Latin: "Sal istud itaque alienæ commistionis expers, acidum est, corporique nostro debita quantitate gratum."</note>. In his *Treatise on Fevers, chapter 15, paragraph 24*: *If indeed the solvents are pleasing to nature, they are willingly admitted within* <note>original Latin: "Si verò dissolventia sint naturæ grata intro lubenter admittuntur."</note>. In his treatise *The Power of Medicines, paragraph 27*: *Therefore, pleasing flavors [to the] stom[ach]* <note>original Latin: "Grati ergo sapores stoma-"; the sentence is interrupted by the page break.</note>

# Van Helmont Revealed

So, after considering all these points of agreement, I do not think one can reasonably doubt this final conjecture: that the last spirit drawn from urine is the third spirit required by Starkey to create his <term>Alkahest<note>The 'Alkahest' was a hypothetical universal solvent sought by alchemists, capable of dissolving any substance into its primary liquid form.</note></term>. But to make the matter even more manifest, and to show that our author has entered into the thought of Van Helmont no less than I have penetrated his own, I shall report this further reason.

Van Helmont, exhorting artists to seek the <term>Alkahest</term>, uses the following words, which we have already partially reported:
> *For first,* he says, *learn to dissolve the Duelech in glass, with a lukewarm liquor, not troublesome to the stomach, nor finally to the bladder: so that the Duelech is gradually diminished without bubbles or agitation: rejoice, for you are near. Then learn to turn the Ludus into salt, etc. (From the treatise 'On Stones,' chapter 7, section 28).* <note>original: "Primum enim... discite dissolvere Duelech in vitro, liquore tepido, non stomacho, non demum vessicæ molesto : sic ut Duelech sensim minuatur absque bullis, & agitatione : gaudete quia propè estis. Tum discite Ludum vertere in salem, &c. de Lith. 7. 28."</note>

But of what use would it be for artists to learn to dissolve the <term>Duelech<note>A term coined by Paracelsus and used by Van Helmont to describe a type of stony concretion or calculus formed in the human body.</note></term>, if that which dissolves it were not some material suitable for forming the <term>Alkahest</term>? Indeed, this is so clearly Van Helmont's thought that he adds: *rejoice, for you are near*; meaning to say, that if they reach that point, they will find themselves—

# Van Helmont Revealed

But if our Author held the opinion—as one cannot doubt—that the Solvent which dissolves the <term>Duelech<note>A term used by Paracelsus and Van Helmont to describe stones formed in the human body, such as kidney or bladder stones.</note></term> was a step toward the discovery of the <term>Alkaest<note>The "universal solvent" sought by alchemists, capable of reducing any body into its primary liquid matter.</note></term>, as indicated by the words "Great Secret" which he adds to the words of Van Helmont: one cannot deny without stubbornness that this Solvent is the Spirit he designates under the name of **non-corrosive Acid**. Indeed, this description does not fit either of the two preceding spirits he described, but on the contrary, fits this third one in every respect.

Having, as I believe, discovered the remote matter and the proximate matter of the Alkaest of Van Helmont or of <insert>George</insert> Starkey <note>An influential 17th-century alchemist and mentor to Robert Boyle; he wrote under the pseudonym Eirenaeus Philalethes.</note>; it remains for me now to point out what may have been the method used by one or the other of these Authors to extract these Spirits from human Urine, and to join them together to form the Liquor they named Alkaest.

If one reads with attention the words of Starkey found at the end of his *Treatise on the Alkaest*, one will easily perceive that the three Spirits of which he composes his Solvent are only visible in the form of two Spirits: one simple and the other double. For when he says that the volatile Spirit of Urine, by means of another spirit entering—

Likewise, Starkey <note>George Starkey (1628–1665), an influential alchemist who wrote under the pseudonym Eirenaeus Philalethes.</note> in his writings has no other goal than the dissolution of a Sal Ammoniac <note>original: "Sel Armoniac"; a chemical salt, typically ammonium chloride, used in various alchemical processes.</note>, which is the result of the mixture of the volatile Spirit and the vinous Spirit of Urine; by means of the corrupted Spirit which he calls the non-corrosive Acid. It is, he says at the end of his *Treatise on the Alkahest* <note>The Alkahest was the theoretical "universal solvent" sought by alchemists.</note>, a spiritual Salt, a Sal Ammoniac—not the common kind, but a Salt formed from the mixture of the sharp, subtle, and penetrating Spirit of human Urine and the mediating or vinous Spirit of the same ferment, in order to be united with a non-corrosive Acid. Our goal, therefore, must be to find in Urine a Spirit that has within itself the means to change into Ammoniac, and a Spirit that can dissolve this Ammoniac and remain inseparably united with it.

For this, obtain a small barrel made of oak wood, new, with a capacity of about thirty pints, which has a bunghole the size of a finger, and a hole at the top of each end of the same size, which must always remain open. Collect in this barrel up to about twenty pints of Urine from young men—healthy, vigorous, of a sanguine temperament <note>In the theory of the four humors, "sanguine" refers to a personality that is energetic and optimistic, associated with an abundance of blood.</note>, who drink only wine, and who are at most twelve to thirteen years old: taking care to close the bunghole every

# The Alkaest or Universal Solvent

Every time you pour the urine into the barrel, as you are able to obtain it, ensure that no foreign matter falls inside. When you have approximately this quantity, you shall place the bunged barrel in some temperate location, and leave the two holes at the top of the ends open so that the air may enter and exit freely, and so that the urine may ferment better therein. After thirty days of fermentation, it will be ready for the work.

Pour into a tall stoneware cucurbite <note>A gourd-shaped glass or ceramic vessel used in distillation; the lower part of an alembic.</note> with a narrow neck about two pints of the fermented urine, and distill about one-third of it using a sand bath <note>A container of sand heated from below to provide even, gentle heat to the vessel.</note> at the first degree <note>The lowest level of heat in alchemy, often compared to the warmth of the sun or a brooding hen.</note>. You shall place this distillate into a large, well-sealed stoneware bottle, and you shall put what remains at the bottom of the cucurbite into another large stoneware bottle. Continue to do the same with the rest of the fermented urine, distilling only two pints at a time and drawing off only one-third at each distillation. Always add this to the large bottle containing what you drew off in the previous distillation; likewise, place what remains at the bottom of the cucurbite after each distillation into the large bottle with the remains from the previous distillation.

-># Van Helmont Revealed<-

Once this is done, pour into the barrel all your remains of **Urines** <note>The "remains" or "feces" of the urine refer to the concentrated liquid left behind after the volatile spirits have been drawn off.</note> which were found at the bottom of the **Cucurbite** <term>Cucurbite: a gourd-shaped glass vessel used in distillation, forming the lower part of an alembic</term> at each distillation, and which you will have stored in one or more large stoneware bottles; close the bung-hole, but leave the other holes open, so that these remains of Urine may ferment once again for another thirty or forty days in the barrel.

Next, **Rectify** <term>Rectification: the process of purifying a liquid through repeated distillation to increase its strength and clarity</term> the **Spirit** <note>In this context, the "Spirit of Urine" refers to an ammonia-rich solution.</note> that you have drawn from all your distillations and which you have set aside in its own bottle, separating it from the **phlegm** <term>Phlegm: the watery, tasteless, and inactive part of a distilled substance</term> as much as you are able by repeated distillations over a first-degree sand heat <note>A "first-degree" heat is the gentlest level of warmth, often compared to the heat of the sun or a brooding hen.</note>, using your tall, narrow-necked Cucurbite: taking only that which rises first, and putting back into the barrel with the urine remains the phlegm that you separate from it at each distillation, which will be found at the bottom of the Cucurbite.

This Spirit, rectified as much as you possibly can, shall be placed in a large **matras** <term>Matras: a long-necked glass flask, also known as a bolt-head, used for digestion and distillation</term> with equal parts of good, perfectly rectified **Spirit of Wine** <note>Spirit of Wine: highly concentrated ethanol or alcohol.</note>; and having agitated them together by shaking the matras, a **white curd** <note>This "white curd" is the *Offa Alba* of Van Helmont, a volatile salt formed by the reaction of ammonia and alcohol.</note> will form, which you will allow to rest for half an hour in order to separate the useless liquid which will float on top. Having separated it, you will pour onto the curd about as much good Spirit...

Your remains of Urine, having fermented for thirty or even forty days, shall be distilled in a tall glass <term>cucurbit</term><note>The lower part of a distilling apparatus, often shaped like a gourd.</note> of the largest size, about two pints at a time, over a <term>sand bath</term><note>A method of heating a vessel evenly by surrounding it with sand.</note> of the first degree. ## APPROBATION.

I, the undersigned, Royal Reader and Professor of Medicine, Doctor, and Regent of the Faculty of Medicine of Paris, have read by order of my Lord the Chancellor this Manuscript entitled: *The Alkahest <note>The "Alkahest" was a hypothetical "universal solvent" sought by alchemists, capable of dissolving any substance into its primary matter.</note> or the Solvent of Van Helmont <note>Jan Baptista van Helmont (1580–1644) was a chemist and physician who claimed to have discovered this secret substance.</note>, revealed in several Treatises which uncover its Secret:* Which appeared to me curious and worthy of being printed. Done at Paris this 24th of September, 1703.

to ensure that the said Petitioner <note>original: "Exposant"; the person applying for the legal privilege</note> or those who shall hold rights from him may enjoy [this privilege] fully and peacefully, without suffering any trouble or hindrance: We will that the Copy which shall be printed at the beginning or at the end of the said Book be given full faith as if it were the Original; and We Command our first Bailiff <note>original: "Huissier"; a court official</note> or Sergeant to perform all required and necessary Acts for the execution of these Presents, without asking for further permission, and notwithstanding any Hue and Cry <note>original: "Clameur de Haro"; an ancient legal appeal to stop an injustice</note>, Norman Charter <note>original: "Chartre Normande"; a set of regional privileges for Normandy</note>, or Letters to the contrary: **FOR SUCH IS OUR PLEASURE.** GIVEN at Versailles, the fourth day of November, in the year of grace one thousand seven hundred and three. And of our Reign the sixty-first.

->By the King in his Council.<-
->Le Comte.<-

*Registered in the Book of the Community of Booksellers and Printers of Paris, Number XXIX. page 34. in accordance with the Regulations, and notably the Decree of the Council of August 13th last. At Paris this Nov. 7, 1703.*
**P. EMERY**, *Syndic <note>An elected official representing the guild of printers and booksellers</note>.*

<summary>This page concludes a royal privilege (copyright) granted at Versailles in 1703, authorizing the printing of a book and warning against any legal interference. It includes the formal registration by the Parisian printers' guild, signed by the syndic P. Emery.</summary>
